========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… honestly, my sleep is kind of why I finally made the appointment. It’s basically fallen apart. I’ll get into bed at my apartment and the laptop is closed, like I’m technically “done” for the night, but my brain just keeps going. I’m lying there for hours mentally rechecking work deadlines, and then tuition payments, and then class assignments, like I’m running through all these lists over and over even though I can’t actually do anything about them at that hour.

And then even when I do fall asleep, it doesn’t really feel like sleep, if that makes sense. I wake up feeling like I never actually turned off. It’s like I was just… paused, but still running in the background the whole night. It’s gotten to the point where I’m coming in because I don’t feel like I can keep functioning like this.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Very anxious. It’s not just like, “Oh, I forgot to do something,” and then I make a note and move on. It’s more like my mind starts grabbing onto one thing and then it immediately turns into ten other things.

Like I’ll think, did I handle that work deadline correctly, am I falling behind, did I miss some detail everyone else noticed, and then somehow that turns into, what if this affects how people see me at work, what if I’m not actually keeping up. And then it jumps to school, like whether I’m wasting money on classes or if tuition is a mistake or if I’m going to end up regretting all of it. And then I start thinking about my family and whether they’ll think I can’t handle my life, or that I’m making bad decisions, or that I’m not as capable as I’m supposed to be.

It’s like every small thing gets inflated into this huge possible disaster. Something that should be minor starts feeling like it could derail my whole future, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but at night it feels completely real.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think I have, though it’s less like I’m consciously saying, “I’m going to avoid this,” and more like things just start dropping off because I’m so caught up in worrying or trying to mentally prepare for everything.

Work is the biggest one. I’m a management analyst, so deadlines are not exactly optional, and lately I’ve actually been missing some of them or cutting things way closer than I ever used to. Then I get more worried about it, so I spend all this time rechecking and second-guessing, and somehow I’m still behind. It’s embarrassing, because I’m usually pretty organized.

And with my certificate classes, I’m falling behind there too. I’ll avoid opening the course site or looking at assignments because I’m already worried I’m not doing enough, and then of course not looking makes it worse. It becomes this whole loop.

I’ve also stopped doing the basic routines that used to help me feel like a normal person, honestly. I used to do yoga, I used to run, I cooked for myself pretty regularly. Now those things feel like too much, or I tell myself I don’t have time because I’m behind on everything, but then I don’t actually use the time well because I’m just worrying. So yes, I guess I am avoiding things, and it’s definitely starting to affect my day-to-day life.

Very anxious. It’s not just like, “Oh, I forgot to do something,” and then I make a note and move on. It’s more like my mind starts grabbing onto one thing and then it immediately turns into ten other things.\n\nLike I’ll think, did I handle that work deadline correctly, am I falling behind, did I miss some detail everyone else noticed, and then somehow that turns into, what if this affects how people see me at work, what if I’m not actually keeping up. I’m a management analyst, so deadlines are not exactly optional, and lately I’ve actually been missing some of them or cutting things way closer than I ever used to. It’s embarrassing, because I’m usually pretty organized.\n\nAnd with my certificate classes, I’m falling behind there too. I’ll avoid opening the course site or looking at assignments because I’m already worried I’m not doing enough, and then of course not looking makes it worse. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think the closest thing to a trigger was when I started my evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as a management analyst. That was about… a year and three months ago now, I think.

At first I told myself it was just going to be a busy season and I could handle it if I stayed organized, because I’d managed work and school before in different ways. But once the evening classes started, it felt like there was never really an “off” part of the day anymore. Work would end, but then school would start, and then if I wasn’t actively doing either one, I was thinking about what I should be doing for one of them.

So I wouldn’t say there was one dramatic event, like one single thing happened and everything changed overnight. It was more like taking on the certificate program on top of the full-time job created this constant pressure, and the worrying just sort of… grew from there and never really went back down. That’s been going on for around fifteen months now.

Then I get more worried about it, so I spend all this time rechecking and second-guessing, and somehow I’m still behind. It’s embarrassing, because I’m usually pretty organized.\n\nAnd with my certificate classes, I’m falling behind there too. I’ll avoid opening the course site or looking at assignments because I’m already worried I’m not doing enough, and then of course not looking makes it worse. It becomes this whole loop.\n\nI’ve also stopped doing the basic routines that used to help me feel like a normal person, honestly. I used to do yoga, I used to run, I cooked for myself pretty regularly. Now those things feel like too much, or I tell myself I don’t have time because I’m behind on everything, but then I don’t actually use the time well because I’m just worrying. So yes, I guess I am avoiding things, and it’s definitely starting to affect my day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a particular event or experience that you think might have triggered these worries and anxiety for you?\n\nPatient: I think the closest thing to a trigger was when I started my evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as a management analyst. That was about… a year and three months ago now, I think.\n\nAt first I told myself it was just going to be a busy season and I could handle it if I stayed organized, because I’d managed work and school before in different ways. But once the evening classes started, it felt like there was never really an “off” part of the day anymore. Work would end, but then school would start, and then if I wasn’t actively doing either one, I was thinking about what I should be doing for one of them.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say there was one dramatic event, like one single thing happened and everything changed overnight. It was more like taking on the certificate program on top of the full-time job created this constant pressure, and the worrying just sort of… grew from there and never really went back down. That’s been going on for around fifteen months now.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs there ever a time when you don't feel anxious or worried, or are you anxious about most things most of the time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / management_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\nmanifestation: \"My mind keeps jumping from whether I’m falling behind at work, to whether I’m wasting money on school, to whether my family will think I can’t handle things, and I treat every small issue like it could derail my whole future.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wish I could say there are clear stretches where I’m not anxious, but honestly it feels like it’s there most of the time, just changing topics.

It’s not always at the exact same intensity, but my mind keeps moving from one worry to the next. Like, I’ll be thinking about whether I’m falling behind at work, and then that turns into whether I’m messing up in a way people are going to notice. Then it shifts to school, like whether I’m wasting money on classes or making a bad decision by doing all of this. And then I’ll start thinking about my family and whether they’re going to think I can’t handle things, or that I’m not as capable as I’m supposed to be.

So it’s not one single worry that comes and goes. It’s more like… if one thing quiets down, my brain finds another thing to latch onto. And even small stuff doesn’t stay small in my head. It starts feeling like it could affect everything, like one missed detail or one bad choice could somehow derail my whole future. I know that sounds extreme, but in the moment it doesn’t feel extreme. It feels like I’m just seeing all the ways things could go wrong.

I’m lying there for hours mentally rechecking work deadlines, and then tuition payments, and then class assignments, like I’m running through all these lists over and over even though I can’t actually do anything about them at that hour.\n\nAnd then even when I do fall asleep, it doesn’t really feel like sleep, if that makes sense. Work would end, but then school would start, and then if I wasn’t actively doing either one, I was thinking about what I should be doing for one of them.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say there was one dramatic event, like one single thing happened and everything changed overnight. It was more like taking on the certificate program on top of the full-time job created this constant pressure, and the worrying just sort of… grew from there and never really went back down. That’s been going on for around fifteen months now.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you don't feel anxious or worried, or are you anxious about most things most of the time?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say there are clear stretches where I’m not anxious, but honestly it feels like it’s there most of the time, just changing topics.\n\nIt’s not always at the exact same intensity, but my mind keeps moving from one worry to the next. Like, I’ll be thinking about whether I’m falling behind at work, and then that turns into whether I’m messing up in a way people are going to notice. Then it shifts to school, like whether I’m wasting money on classes or making a bad decision by doing all of this. And then I’ll start thinking about my family and whether they’re going to think I can’t handle things, or that I’m not as capable as I’m supposed to be.\n\nSo it’s not one single worry that comes and goes. It’s more like… if one thing quiets down, my brain finds another thing to latch onto. And even small stuff doesn’t stay small in my head. It starts feeling like it could affect everything, like one missed detail or one bad choice could somehow derail my whole future. I know that sounds extreme, but in the moment it doesn’t feel extreme. It feels like I’m just seeing all the ways things could go wrong.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you avoid social interactions or talking to people because you're worried about what they might think of you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / management_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure. I mean… I do worry what people think of me, definitely, especially if I feel like I’m not on top of things or I might come across badly. But I don’t know if I’d say I’m specifically avoiding social interactions because of that.

It’s more like sometimes talking to people feels like another thing I have to manage, if that makes sense. Like I have to seem okay, or explain why I haven’t kept up with something, or not let on that I’m kind of overwhelmed. So I might put off replying to a message or avoid starting a conversation, but I’m not sure if that’s because I’m afraid they’ll judge me, or because I just don’t have the mental energy for it.

So… yes and no, I guess. I haven’t really thought of it as social avoidance exactly. It’s more that I get tired of trying to sound normal or competent, and then I pull back a little.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… honestly, my sleep is kind of why I finally made the appointment. It’s basically fallen apart. I’ll get into bed at my apartment and the laptop is closed, like I’m technically “done” for the night, but my brain just keeps going. I’m lying there for hours mentally rechecking work deadlines, and then tuition payments, and then class assignments, like I’m running through all these lists over and over even though I can’t actually do anything about them at that hour.\n\nAnd then even when I do fall asleep, it doesn’t really feel like sleep, if that makes sense. I wake up feeling like I never actually turned off. It’s like I was just… paused, but still running in the background the whole night. It’s gotten to the point where I’m coming in because I don’t feel like I can keep functioning like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you're lying in bed and your brain starts racing through those lists?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very anxious. It’s not just like, “Oh, I forgot to do something,” and then I make a note and move on. It’s more like my mind starts grabbing onto one thing and then it immediately turns into ten other things.\n\nLike I’ll think, did I handle that work deadline correctly, am I falling behind, did I miss some detail everyone else noticed, and then somehow that turns into, what if this affects how people see me at work, what if I’m not actually keeping up. And then it jumps to school, like whether I’m wasting money on classes or if tuition is a mistake or if I’m going to end up regretting all of it. And then I start thinking about my family and whether they’ll think I can’t handle my life, or that I’m making bad decisions, or that I’m not as capable as I’m supposed to be.\n\nIt’s like every small thing gets inflated into this huge possible disaster. Something that should be minor starts feeling like it could derail my whole future, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but at night it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, though it’s less like I’m consciously saying, “I’m going to avoid this,” and more like things just start dropping off because I’m so caught up in worrying or trying to mentally prepare for everything.\n\nWork is the biggest one. I’m a management analyst, so deadlines are not exactly optional, and lately I’ve actually been missing some of them or cutting things way closer than I ever used to. Then I get more worried about it, so I spend all this time rechecking and second-guessing, and somehow I’m still behind. It’s embarrassing, because I’m usually pretty organized.\n\nAnd with my certificate classes, I’m falling behind there too. I’ll avoid opening the course site or looking at assignments because I’m already worried I’m not doing enough, and then of course not looking makes it worse. It becomes this whole loop.\n\nI’ve also stopped doing the basic routines that used to help me feel like a normal person, honestly. I used to do yoga, I used to run, I cooked for myself pretty regularly. Now those things feel like too much, or I tell myself I don’t have time because I’m behind on everything, but then I don’t actually use the time well because I’m just worrying. So yes, I guess I am avoiding things, and it’s definitely starting to affect my day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a particular event or experience that you think might have triggered these worries and anxiety for you?\n\nPatient: I think the closest thing to a trigger was when I started my evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as a management analyst. It was more like taking on the certificate program on top of the full-time job created this constant pressure, and the worrying just sort of… grew from there and never really went back down. That’s been going on for around fifteen months now.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you don't feel anxious or worried, or are you anxious about most things most of the time?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say there are clear stretches where I’m not anxious, but honestly it feels like it’s there most of the time, just changing topics.\n\nIt’s not always at the exact same intensity, but my mind keeps moving from one worry to the next. Like, I’ll be thinking about whether I’m falling behind at work, and then that turns into whether I’m messing up in a way people are going to notice. Then it shifts to school, like whether I’m wasting money on classes or making a bad decision by doing all of this. And then I’ll start thinking about my family and whether they’re going to think I can’t handle things, or that I’m not as capable as I’m supposed to be.\n\nSo it’s not one single worry that comes and goes. It’s more like… if one thing quiets down, my brain finds another thing to latch onto. And even small stuff doesn’t stay small in my head. It starts feeling like it could affect everything, like one missed detail or one bad choice could somehow derail my whole future. I know that sounds extreme, but in the moment it doesn’t feel extreme. It feels like I’m just seeing all the ways things could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social interactions or talking to people because you're worried about what they might think of you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, I’ll be thinking about whether I’m falling behind at work, and then that turns into whether I’m messing up in a way people are going to notice. Then it shifts to school, like whether I’m wasting money on classes or making a bad decision by doing all of this. And then I’ll start thinking about my family and whether they’re going to think I can’t handle things, or that I’m not as capable as I’m supposed to be.\n\nSo it’s not one single worry that comes and goes. It’s more like… if one thing quiets down, my brain finds another thing to latch onto. And even small stuff doesn’t stay small in my head. It starts feeling like it could affect everything, like one missed detail or one bad choice could somehow derail my whole future. I know that sounds extreme, but in the moment it doesn’t feel extreme. It feels like I’m just seeing all the ways things could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social interactions or talking to people because you're worried about what they might think of you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean… I do worry what people think of me, definitely, especially if I feel like I’m not on top of things or I might come across badly. But I don’t know if I’d say I’m specifically avoiding social interactions because of that.\n\nIt’s more like sometimes talking to people feels like another thing I have to manage, if that makes sense. Like I have to seem okay, or explain why I haven’t kept up with something, or not let on that I’m kind of overwhelmed. So I might put off replying to a message or avoid starting a conversation, but I’m not sure if that’s because I’m afraid they’ll judge me, or because I just don’t have the mental energy for it.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I haven’t really thought of it as social avoidance exactly. It’s more that I get tired of trying to sound normal or competent, and then I pull back a little.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs there anything in particular that you worry about most frequently?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / management_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\nmanifestation: \"My mind keeps jumping from whether I’m falling behind at work, to whether I’m wasting money on school, to whether my family will think I can’t handle things, and I treat every small issue like it could derail my whole future.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Mostly work and school, but it doesn’t stay neatly in one category. It’s like my mind keeps jumping around between them and then connecting everything to some bigger consequence.

At work, I worry I’m falling behind or missing something obvious, and then it turns into, “What if people notice I’m not doing enough?” or “What if this means I’m not actually good at my job?” Then with school, I worry I’m spending all this money and time and maybe I made the wrong choice, or I’m not keeping up enough to justify it. And then somehow that turns into worrying about what my family would think if I couldn’t handle it, like they’d see me as less capable than they thought.

The worst part is that small things don’t feel small. Like one email I haven’t answered, one assignment I’m unsure about, one awkward conversation — my brain treats it like it could snowball and affect my whole future. I know when I say it out loud, it sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real, like I’m just trying to prevent everything from falling apart.

I’m lying there for hours mentally rechecking work deadlines, and then tuition payments, and then class assignments, like I’m running through all these lists over and over even though I can’t actually do anything about them at that hour.\n\nAnd then even when I do fall asleep, it doesn’t really feel like sleep, if that makes sense. I wake up feeling like I never actually turned off. It’s like I was just… paused, but still running in the background the whole night. Very anxious. It’s not just like, “Oh, I forgot to do something,” and then I make a note and move on. Something that should be minor starts feeling like it could derail my whole future, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but at night it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, though it’s less like I’m consciously saying, “I’m going to avoid this,” and more like things just start dropping off because I’m so caught up in worrying or trying to mentally prepare for everything.\n\nWork is the biggest one. I’m a management analyst, so deadlines are not exactly optional, and lately I’ve actually been missing some of them or cutting things way closer than I ever used to. Then I get more worried about it, so I spend all this time rechecking and second-guessing, and somehow I’m still behind. It’s embarrassing, because I’m usually pretty organized.\n\nAnd with my certificate classes, I’m falling behind there too. I’ll avoid opening the course site or looking at assignments because I’m already worried I’m not doing enough, and then of course not looking makes it worse. It becomes this whole loop.\n\nI’ve also stopped doing the basic routines that used to help me feel like a normal person, honestly. I used to do yoga, I used to run, I cooked for myself pretty regularly. Work would end, but then school would start, and then if I wasn’t actively doing either one, I was thinking about what I should be doing for one of them.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say there was one dramatic event, like one single thing happened and everything changed overnight. It was more like taking on the certificate program on top of the full-time job created this constant pressure, and the worrying just sort of… grew from there and never really went back down. It feels like I’m just seeing all the ways things could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social interactions or talking to people because you're worried about what they might think of you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly… not much comes to mind. I don’t mean that in a dramatic way, like literally every single object or topic scares me, but it’s more that almost anything can become something I worry about if my brain can connect it to a bigger consequence.

Like, even if it starts with something ordinary — an email I haven’t answered, or a school expense, or a conversation with my family — it doesn’t stay contained. My mind jumps from, “Am I falling behind at work?” to “What if I’m not actually doing well enough?” Then it goes to school, like, “Am I wasting money? Did I make the wrong decision?” And then somehow it becomes, “What will my family think if I can’t handle all of this?”

So when you ask what I don’t worry about… I guess I can’t really give you a clean category. It feels like if something matters even a little, my mind can turn it into proof that my whole future might fall apart. Even small things feel like they could derail everything if I don’t catch them in time.

Something that should be minor starts feeling like it could derail my whole future, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but at night it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, though it’s less like I’m consciously saying, “I’m going to avoid this,” and more like things just start dropping off because I’m so caught up in worrying or trying to mentally prepare for everything.\n\nWork is the biggest one. my whole future.\n- key: S057\n  name: Uncontrollable_Worry\n  description: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.\n  manifestation: I try to tell myself to stop and focus on one thing, but one worry immediately leads to another until I’m checking my calendar, my budget, and my assignments over and over instead of actually getting anything done.\n- key: S059\n  name: Concentration_Difficulty\n  description: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.\n  manifestation: I’ll stare at the same financial model or class reading for ages and realize I’ve reread the same paragraph five times without absorbing it, because my head is already off running through everything that could go wrong.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve started missing work deadlines and falling behind in my certificate classes, and I’ve stopped doing the routines that used to keep me grounded, like yoga, running, and cooking.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and three months, since I started the evening graduate certificate program while keeping my full-time analyst job.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / management_analyst / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S027\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you avoid social interactions or talking to people because you're worried about what they might think of you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean… I do worry what people think of me, definitely, especially if I feel like I’m not on top of things or I might come across badly. But I don’t know if I’d say I’m specifically avoiding social interactions because of that.\n\nIt’s more like sometimes talking to people feels like another thing I have to manage, if that makes sense. Like I have to seem okay, or explain why I haven’t kept up with something, or not let on that I’m kind of overwhelmed. So I might put off replying to a message or avoid starting a conversation, but I’m not sure if that’s because I’m afraid they’ll judge me, or because I just don’t have the mental energy for it.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I haven’t really thought of it as social avoidance exactly. It’s more that I get tired of trying to sound normal or competent, and then I pull back a little.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that you worry about most frequently?\n\nPatient: Mostly work and school, but it doesn’t stay neatly in one category. It’s like my mind keeps jumping around between them and then connecting everything to some bigger consequence.\n\nAt work, I worry I’m falling behind or missing something obvious, and then it turns into, “What if people notice I’m not doing enough?” or “What if this means I’m not actually good at my job?” Then with school, I worry I’m spending all this money and time and maybe I made the wrong choice, or I’m not keeping up enough to justify it. And then somehow that turns into worrying about what my family would think if I couldn’t handle it, like they’d see me as less capable than they thought.\n\nThe worst part is that small things don’t feel small. Like one email I haven’t answered, one assignment I’m unsure about, one awkward conversation — my brain treats it like it could snowball and affect my whole future. I know when I say it out loud, it sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real, like I’m just trying to prevent everything from falling apart.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you don't worry about?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not much comes to mind. I don’t mean that in a dramatic way, like literally every single object or topic scares me, but it’s more that almost anything can become something I worry about if my brain can connect it to a bigger consequence.\n\nLike, even if it starts with something ordinary — an email I haven’t answered, or a school expense, or a conversation with my family — it doesn’t stay contained. My mind jumps from, “Am I falling behind at work?” to “What if I’m not actually doing well enough?” Then it goes to school, like, “Am I wasting money? Did I make the wrong decision?” And then somehow it becomes, “What will my family think if I can’t handle all of this?”\n\nSo when you ask what I don’t worry about… I guess I can’t really give you a clean category. It feels like if something matters even a little, my mind can turn it into proof that my whole future might fall apart. Even small things feel like they could derail everything if I don’t catch them in time.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your anxiety mainly about work, school, and how others perceive you, or does it also include other areas of your life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s mainly those things, but it doesn’t really stay limited to them. Work and school are probably the easiest examples because they’re constant, and they feel like they have obvious consequences attached to them. Like if I’m behind at work, then my mind goes to, “What if I’m not doing enough?” or “What if people realize I can’t keep up?” And with school it turns into, “Am I wasting money? Did I make the wrong decision? What if I can’t handle this?”

But then it spreads into other areas too, especially family, or just my future in general. I’ll start with something small, like one task I haven’t done or one conversation I’m putting off, and somehow my brain makes it part of this bigger chain of consequences. Like, if I mess this up, then maybe my family will think I’m not as capable as they thought, or maybe this means I’m going to fall apart later.

So I guess the core is work, school, and being seen as capable, but it doesn’t feel contained. It’s more like my mind keeps jumping from one thing to the next, and every small issue starts to feel like it could derail my whole future if I don’t handle it perfectly.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… honestly, my sleep is kind of why I finally made the appointment. It’s basically fallen apart. I’ll get into bed at my apartment and the laptop is closed, like I’m technically “done” for the night, but my brain just keeps going. I’m lying there for hours mentally rechecking work deadlines, and then tuition payments, and then class assignments, like I’m running through all these lists over and over even though I can’t actually do anything about them at that hour.\n\nAnd then even when I do fall asleep, it doesn’t really feel like sleep, if that makes sense. I wake up feeling like I never actually turned off. It’s like I was just… paused, but still running in the background the whole night. It’s gotten to the point where I’m coming in because I don’t feel like I can keep functioning like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you're lying in bed and your brain starts racing through those lists?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very anxious. It’s not just like, “Oh, I forgot to do something,” and then I make a note and move on. It’s more like my mind starts grabbing onto one thing and then it immediately turns into ten other things.\n\nLike I’ll think, did I handle that work deadline correctly, am I falling behind, did I miss some detail everyone else noticed, and then somehow that turns into, what if this affects how people see me at work, what if I’m not actually keeping up. And then it jumps to school, like whether I’m wasting money on classes or if tuition is a mistake or if I’m going to end up regretting all of it. And then I start thinking about my family and whether they’ll think I can’t handle my life, or that I’m making bad decisions, or that I’m not as capable as I’m supposed to be.\n\nIt’s like every small thing gets inflated into this huge possible disaster. Something that should be minor starts feeling like it could derail my whole future, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but at night it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, though it’s less like I’m consciously saying, “I’m going to avoid this,” and more like things just start dropping off because I’m so caught up in worrying or trying to mentally prepare for everything.\n\nWork is the biggest one. I’m a management analyst, so deadlines are not exactly optional, and lately I’ve actually been missing some of them or cutting things way closer than I ever used to. Then I get more worried about it, so I spend all this time rechecking and second-guessing, and somehow I’m still behind. It’s embarrassing, because I’m usually pretty organized.\n\nAnd with my certificate classes, I’m falling behind there too. I’ll avoid opening the course site or looking at assignments because I’m already worried I’m not doing enough, and then of course not looking makes it worse. It becomes this whole loop.\n\nI’ve also stopped doing the basic routines that used to help me feel like a normal person, honestly. I used to do yoga, I used to run, I cooked for myself pretty regularly. Now those things feel like too much, or I tell myself I don’t have time because I’m behind on everything, but then I don’t actually use the time well because I’m just worrying. So yes, I guess I am avoiding things, and it’s definitely starting to affect my day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a particular event or experience that you think might have triggered these worries and anxiety for you?\n\nPatient: I think the closest thing to a trigger was when I started my evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as a management analyst. That was about… a year and three months ago now, I think.\n\nAt first I told myself it was just going to be a busy season and I could handle it if I stayed organized, because I’d managed work and school before in different ways. But once the evening classes started, it felt like there was never really an “off” part of the day anymore. Work would end, but then school would start, and then if I wasn’t actively doing either one, I was thinking about what I should be doing for one of them.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say there was one dramatic event, like one single thing happened and everything changed overnight. It was more like taking on the certificate program on top of the full-time job created this constant pressure, and the worrying just sort of… grew from there and never really went back down. That’s been going on for around fifteen months now.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you don't feel anxious or worried, or are you anxious about most things most of the time?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say there are clear stretches where I’m not anxious, but honestly it feels like it’s there most of the time, just changing topics.\n\nIt’s not always at the exact same intensity, but my mind keeps moving from one worry to the next. Like, I’ll be thinking about whether I’m falling behind at work, and then that turns into whether I’m messing up in a way people are going to notice. Then it shifts to school, like whether I’m wasting money on classes or making a bad decision by doing all of this. And then I’ll start thinking about my family and whether they’re going to think I can’t handle things, or that I’m not as capable as I’m supposed to be.\n\nSo it’s not one single worry that comes and goes. It’s more like… if one thing quiets down, my brain finds another thing to latch onto. And even small stuff doesn’t stay small in my head. It starts feeling like it could affect everything, like one missed detail or one bad choice could somehow derail my whole future. I know that sounds extreme, but in the moment it doesn’t feel extreme. It feels like I’m just seeing all the ways things could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social interactions or talking to people because you're worried about what they might think of you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean… I do worry what people think of me, definitely, especially if I feel like I’m not on top of things or I might come across badly. But I don’t know if I’d say I’m specifically avoiding social interactions because of that.\n\nIt’s more like sometimes talking to people feels like another thing I have to manage, if that makes sense. Like I have to seem okay, or explain why I haven’t kept up with something, or not let on that I’m kind of overwhelmed. So I might put off replying to a message or avoid starting a conversation, but I’m not sure if that’s because I’m afraid they’ll judge me, or because I just don’t have the mental energy for it.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I haven’t really thought of it as social avoidance exactly. It’s more that I get tired of trying to sound normal or competent, and then I pull back a little.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that you worry about most frequently?\n\nPatient: Mostly work and school, but it doesn’t stay neatly in one category. It’s like my mind keeps jumping around between them and then connecting everything to some bigger consequence.\n\nAt work, I worry I’m falling behind or missing something obvious, and then it turns into, “What if people notice I’m not doing enough?” or “What if this means I’m not actually good at my job?” Then with school, I worry I’m spending all this money and time and maybe I made the wrong choice, or I’m not keeping up enough to justify it. And then somehow that turns into worrying about what my family would think if I couldn’t handle it, like they’d see me as less capable than they thought.\n\nThe worst part is that small things don’t feel small. Like one email I haven’t answered, one assignment I’m unsure about, one awkward conversation — my brain treats it like it could snowball and affect my whole future. I know when I say it out loud, it sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real, like I’m just trying to prevent everything from falling apart.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you don't worry about?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not much comes to mind. I don’t mean that in a dramatic way, like literally every single object or topic scares me, but it’s more that almost anything can become something I worry about if my brain can connect it to a bigger consequence.\n\nLike, even if it starts with something ordinary — an email I haven’t answered, or a school expense, or a conversation with my family — it doesn’t stay contained. My mind jumps from, “Am I falling behind at work?” to “What if I’m not actually doing well enough?” Then it goes to school, like, “Am I wasting money? Did I make the wrong decision?” And then somehow it becomes, “What will my family think if I can’t handle all of this?”\n\nSo when you ask what I don’t worry about… I guess I can’t really give you a clean category. It feels like if something matters even a little, my mind can turn it into proof that my whole future might fall apart. Even small things feel like they could derail everything if I don’t catch them in time.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mainly about work, school, and how others perceive you, or does it also include other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly those things, but it doesn’t really stay limited to them. Work and school are probably the easiest examples because they’re constant, and they feel like they have obvious consequences attached to them. Like if I’m behind at work, then my mind goes to, “What if I’m not doing enough?” or “What if people realize I can’t keep up?” And with school it turns into, “Am I wasting money? Did I make the wrong decision? What if I can’t handle this?”\n\nBut then it spreads into other areas too, especially family, or just my future in general. I’ll start with something small, like one task I haven’t done or one conversation I’m putting off, and somehow my brain makes it part of this bigger chain of consequences. Like, if I mess this up, then maybe my family will think I’m not as capable as they thought, or maybe this means I’m going to fall apart later.\n\nSo I guess the core is work, school, and being seen as capable, but it doesn’t feel contained. It’s more like my mind keeps jumping from one thing to the next, and every small issue starts to feel like it could derail my whole future if I don’t handle it perfectly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It starts feeling like it could affect everything, like one missed detail or one bad choice could somehow derail my whole future. I know that sounds extreme, but in the moment it doesn’t feel extreme. It feels like I’m just seeing all the ways things could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social interactions or talking to people because you're worried about what they might think of you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean… I do worry what people think of me, definitely, especially if I feel like I’m not on top of things or I might come across badly. But I don’t know if I’d say I’m specifically avoiding social interactions because of that.\n\nIt’s more like sometimes talking to people feels like another thing I have to manage, if that makes sense. Like I have to seem okay, or explain why I haven’t kept up with something, or not let on that I’m kind of overwhelmed. So I might put off replying to a message or avoid starting a conversation, but I’m not sure if that’s because I’m afraid they’ll judge me, or because I just don’t have the mental energy for it.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I haven’t really thought of it as social avoidance exactly. It’s more that I get tired of trying to sound normal or competent, and then I pull back a little.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that you worry about most frequently?\n\nPatient: Mostly work and school, but it doesn’t stay neatly in one category. It’s like my mind keeps jumping around between them and then connecting everything to some bigger consequence.\n\nAt work, I worry I’m falling behind or missing something obvious, and then it turns into, “What if people notice I’m not doing enough?” or “What if this means I’m not actually good at my job?” Then with school, I worry I’m spending all this money and time and maybe I made the wrong choice, or I’m not keeping up enough to justify it. And then somehow that turns into worrying about what my family would think if I couldn’t handle it, like they’d see me as less capable than they thought.\n\nThe worst part is that small things don’t feel small. Like one email I haven’t answered, one assignment I’m unsure about, one awkward conversation — my brain treats it like it could snowball and affect my whole future. I know when I say it out loud, it sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real, like I’m just trying to prevent everything from falling apart.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you don't worry about?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not much comes to mind. I don’t mean that in a dramatic way, like literally every single object or topic scares me, but it’s more that almost anything can become something I worry about if my brain can connect it to a bigger consequence.\n\nLike, even if it starts with something ordinary — an email I haven’t answered, or a school expense, or a conversation with my family — it doesn’t stay contained. My mind jumps from, “Am I falling behind at work?” to “What if I’m not actually doing well enough?” Then it goes to school, like, “Am I wasting money? Did I make the wrong decision?” And then somehow it becomes, “What will my family think if I can’t handle all of this?”\n\nSo when you ask what I don’t worry about… I guess I can’t really give you a clean category. It feels like if something matters even a little, my mind can turn it into proof that my whole future might fall apart. Even small things feel like they could derail everything if I don’t catch them in time.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mainly about work, school, and how others perceive you, or does it also include other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly those things, but it doesn’t really stay limited to them. Work and school are probably the easiest examples because they’re constant, and they feel like they have obvious consequences attached to them. Like if I’m behind at work, then my mind goes to, “What if I’m not doing enough?” or “What if people realize I can’t keep up?” And with school it turns into, “Am I wasting money? Did I make the wrong decision? What if I can’t handle this?”\n\nBut then it spreads into other areas too, especially family, or just my future in general. I’ll start with something small, like one task I haven’t done or one conversation I’m putting off, and somehow my brain makes it part of this bigger chain of consequences. Like, if I mess this up, then maybe my family will think I’m not as capable as they thought, or maybe this means I’m going to fall apart later.\n\nSo I guess the core is work, school, and being seen as capable, but it doesn’t feel contained. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do. I mean, work and school are the easiest things to point to, because they’re obvious stressors, but even when I’m not directly thinking about them, my mind kind of finds another doorway into the same worry.

Like I could be doing something completely unrelated, and then one small thing will remind me of something I haven’t handled, or something I’m unsure about, and suddenly I’m back in that loop. It’ll go from, “Am I falling behind at work?” to “Am I wasting money on school?” to “What if my family thinks I can’t handle things?” And even if the original situation had nothing to do with work or school, it somehow becomes connected to this bigger fear that I’m not managing my life well enough.

So yes, it’s not like I only feel anxious when I’m sitting at my desk or doing schoolwork. It follows me into other situations because I start treating every little issue like it could turn into something huge, like it could derail my whole future if I don’t figure it out right away.

I’ll avoid opening the course site or looking at assignments because I’m already worried I’m not doing enough, and then of course not looking makes it worse. It becomes this whole loop.\n\nI’ve also stopped doing the basic routines that used to help me feel like a normal person, honestly. I used to do yoga, I used to run, I cooked for myself pretty regularly. Now those things feel like too much, or I tell myself I don’t have time because I’m behind on everything, but then I don’t actually use the time well because I’m just worrying. So yes, I guess I am avoiding things, and it’s definitely starting to affect my day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a particular event or experience that you think might have triggered these worries and anxiety for you?\n\nPatient: I think the closest thing to a trigger was when I started my evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as a management analyst. That was about… a year and three months ago now, I think.\n\nAt first I told myself it was just going to be a busy season and I could handle it if I stayed organized, because I’d managed work and school before in different ways. But once the evening classes started, it felt like there was never really an “off” part of the day anymore. Work would end, but then school would start, and then if I wasn’t actively doing either one, I was thinking about what I should be doing for one of them.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say there was one dramatic event, like one single thing happened and everything changed overnight. It was more like taking on the certificate program on top of the full-time job created this constant pressure, and the worrying just sort of… grew from there and never really went back down. That’s been going on for around fifteen months now.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you don't feel anxious or worried, or are you anxious about most things most of the time?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say there are clear stretches where I’m not anxious, but honestly it feels like it’s there most of the time, just changing topics.\n\nIt’s not always at the exact same intensity, but my mind keeps moving from one worry to the next. Like, I’ll be thinking about whether I’m falling behind at work, and then that turns into whether I’m messing up in a way people are going to notice. Then it shifts to school, like whether I’m wasting money on classes or making a bad decision by doing all of this. And then I’ll start thinking about my family and whether they’re going to think I can’t handle things, or that I’m not as capable as I’m supposed to be.\n\nSo it’s not one single worry that comes and goes. It’s more like… if one thing quiets down, my brain finds another thing to latch onto. And even small stuff doesn’t stay small in my head. It starts feeling like it could affect everything, like one missed detail or one bad choice could somehow derail my whole future. I know that sounds extreme, but in the moment it doesn’t feel extreme. It feels like I’m just seeing all the ways things could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social interactions or talking to people because you're worried about what they might think of you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean… I do worry what people think of me, definitely, especially if I feel like I’m not on top of things or I might come across badly. But I don’t know if I’d say I’m specifically avoiding social interactions because of that.\n\nIt’s more like sometimes talking to people feels like another thing I have to manage, if that makes sense. Like I have to seem okay, or explain why I haven’t kept up with something, or not let on that I’m kind of overwhelmed. So I might put off replying to a message or avoid starting a conversation, but I’m not sure if that’s because I’m afraid they’ll judge me, or because I just don’t have the mental energy for it.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I haven’t really thought of it as social avoidance exactly. It’s more that I get tired of trying to sound normal or competent, and then I pull back a little.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that you worry about most frequently?\n\nPatient: Mostly work and school, but it doesn’t stay neatly in one category. It’s like my mind keeps jumping around between them and then connecting everything to some bigger consequence.\n\nAt work, I worry I’m falling behind or missing something obvious, and then it turns into, “What if people notice I’m not doing enough?” or “What if this means I’m not actually good at my job?” Then with school, I worry I’m spending all this money and time and maybe I made the wrong choice, or I’m not keeping up enough to justify it. And then somehow that turns into worrying about what my family would think if I couldn’t handle it, like they’d see me as less capable than they thought.\n\nThe worst part is that small things don’t feel small. Like one email I haven’t answered, one assignment I’m unsure about, one awkward conversation — my brain treats it like it could snowball and affect my whole future. I know when I say it out loud, it sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real, like I’m just trying to prevent everything from falling apart.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you don't worry about?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not much comes to mind. I don’t mean that in a dramatic way, like literally every single object or topic scares me, but it’s more that almost anything can become something I worry about if my brain can connect it to a bigger consequence.\n\nLike, even if it starts with something ordinary — an email I haven’t answered, or a school expense, or a conversation with my family — it doesn’t stay contained. My mind jumps from, “Am I falling behind at work?” to “What if I’m not actually doing well enough?” Then it goes to school, like, “Am I wasting money? Did I make the wrong decision?” And then somehow it becomes, “What will my family think if I can’t handle all of this?”\n\nSo when you ask what I don’t worry about… I guess I can’t really give you a clean category. It feels like if something matters even a little, my mind can turn it into proof that my whole future might fall apart. Even small things feel like they could derail everything if I don’t catch them in time.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mainly about work, school, and how others perceive you, or does it also include other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly those things, but it doesn’t really stay limited to them. Work and school are probably the easiest examples because they’re constant, and they feel like they have obvious consequences attached to them. Like if I’m behind at work, then my mind goes to, “What if I’m not doing enough?” or “What if people realize I can’t keep up?” And with school it turns into, “Am I wasting money? Did I make the wrong decision? What if I can’t handle this?”\n\nBut then it spreads into other areas too, especially family, or just my future in general. I’ll start with something small, like one task I haven’t done or one conversation I’m putting off, and somehow my brain makes it part of this bigger chain of consequences. Like, if I mess this up, then maybe my family will think I’m not as capable as they thought, or maybe this means I’m going to fall apart later.\n\nSo I guess the core is work, school, and being seen as capable, but it doesn’t feel contained. It’s more like my mind keeps jumping from one thing to the next, and every small issue starts to feel like it could derail my whole future if I don’t handle it perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people usually mean by trauma, at least. I haven’t had some single major event that I immediately think of when you ask that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had stressful periods and things that felt really overwhelming at the time, but I don’t know that I would call them traumatic. Sometimes I worry I’m using the wrong definition, though, because I know people can mean different things by that. But no, nothing specific comes to mind like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in situations where you're not thinking about work or school?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, work and school are the easiest things to point to, because they’re obvious stressors, but even when I’m not directly thinking about them, my mind kind of finds another doorway into the same worry.\n\nLike I could be doing something completely unrelated, and then one small thing will remind me of something I haven’t handled, or something I’m unsure about, and suddenly I’m back in that loop. It’ll go from, “Am I falling behind at work?” to “Am I wasting money on school?” to “What if my family thinks I can’t handle things?” And even if the original situation had nothing to do with work or school, it somehow becomes connected to this bigger fear that I’m not managing my life well enough.\n\nSo yes, it’s not like I only feel anxious when I’m sitting at my desk or doing schoolwork. It follows me into other situations because I start treating every little issue like it could turn into something huge, like it could derail my whole future if I don’t figure it out right away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Like if I’m behind at work, then my mind goes to, “What if I’m not doing enough?” or “What if people realize I can’t keep up?” And with school it turns into, “Am I wasting money? Did I make the wrong decision? What if I can’t handle this?”\n\nBut then it spreads into other areas too, especially family, or just my future in general. I’ll start with something small, like one task I haven’t done or one conversation I’m putting off, and somehow my brain makes it part of this bigger chain of consequences. Like, if I mess this up, then maybe my family will think I’m not as capable as they thought, or maybe this means I’m going to fall apart later.\n\nSo I guess the core is work, school, and being seen as capable, but it doesn’t feel contained. It’s more like my mind keeps jumping from one thing to the next, and every small issue starts to feel like it could derail my whole future if I don’t handle it perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people usually mean by trauma, at least. I haven’t had some single major event that I immediately think of when you ask that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had stressful periods and things that felt really overwhelming at the time, but I don’t know that I would call them traumatic. Sometimes I worry I’m using the wrong definition, though, because I know people can mean different things by that. But no, nothing specific comes to mind like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in situations where you're not thinking about work or school?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, work and school are the easiest things to point to, because they’re obvious stressors, but even when I’m not directly thinking about them, my mind kind of finds another doorway into the same worry.\n\nLike I could be doing something completely unrelated, and then one small thing will remind me of something I haven’t handled, or something I’m unsure about, and suddenly I’m back in that loop. It’ll go from, “Am I falling behind at work?” to “Am I wasting money on school?” to “What if my family thinks I can’t handle things?” And even if the original situation had nothing to do with work or school, it somehow becomes connected to this bigger fear that I’m not managing my life well enough.\n\nSo yes, it’s not like I only feel anxious when I’m sitting at my desk or doing schoolwork. It follows me into other situations because I start treating every little issue like it could turn into something huge, like it could derail my whole future if I don’t figure it out right away.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel anxious in situations that have nothing to do with work, school, or your family's opinion of you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / management_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\nmanifestation: \"My mind keeps jumping from whether I’m falling behind at work, to whether I’m wasting money on school, to whether my family will think I can’t handle things, and I treat every small issue like it could derail my whole future.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so, although it’s hard for me to separate it cleanly because my brain tends to connect everything back to those things eventually.

Like even if the situation itself has nothing to do with work or school or my family, I can start worrying from something really small. It could be some random errand, or a decision I have to make, or just noticing that I haven’t handled something yet, and then my mind starts jumping. It’s like it doesn’t stay with the actual thing in front of me. It turns into, “Okay, if I can’t handle this small thing, what does that say about everything else?” And then before I know it, I’m thinking about whether I’m falling behind at work, or whether school was a mistake and I’m wasting money, or whether people in my family are going to think I can’t manage my life.

So yes, I do feel anxious in situations that technically have nothing to do with those areas. It’s just that my mind kind of pulls them in anyway. Every small issue starts feeling like it has this huge meaning, like if I don’t solve it perfectly or quickly enough, it could somehow derail my whole future.
